Yeah… I do. That’s kind of one of the bigger reasons I came in, actually.

I keep getting stuck on this feeling like I’ve ruined things for everybody, even when, if someone else did the same thing, I’d probably tell them, “Just fix it and move on.” But when it’s me, it feels like proof I shouldn’t be there. Like I’m fake in the apprenticeship, like everyone else belongs and I’m just waiting for them to notice I don’t.

And then I think about my family, or even the younger apprentices watching what I do, and I feel like I’m letting them down. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because it’s not like one mistake ends the world. But it feels that way in my head sometimes.

Yeah… I think so. It’s weird because I still know I used to like those things, but lately I’ll walk right past my sketchbook and just not pick it up. And volleyball too — I’ll tell myself I should go, because once I’m there it’s usually good, or it used to be, anyway, but then I just… don’t.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, a little. Mostly at work I notice it. Like I’ll be on a job and something simple—climbing down to grab a fitting or answering the foreman when he asks me something—feels like it takes way more effort than it should.

And it’s embarrassing because plumbing stuff already has this rhythm to it, you know, like everyone’s moving around and you don’t want to be the one slowing it down. I’ll catch myself just… moving slow. Not on purpose. And a couple people have asked if I’m sick or hungover, which I hated, because I’m like, no, I’m just trying to get through the morning.

So yeah, not every second, but enough that other people have noticed.

I know other people would probably just fix the issue and move on, but I get stuck on it like it means something about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not good enough or that you’ve let others down more than you actually have, on most days lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days lately, I think. That’s kind of what made me come in, honestly. I keep getting stuck in this thing where I feel like I ruined stuff for everybody, even when it’s not that big.\n\nLike at the apprenticeship, if I mess something up, I start feeling like I’m a fake and I shouldn’t even be there. And then my brain goes off into, like, “you let your family down,” or even the younger apprentices, which doesn’t totally make sense because they’re not sitting there saying that to me. I know someone else would probably just fix it and move on, but I don’t. I just carry it around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the time, nearly every day, for as long as you can remember—like, has this been going on for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No, not two years or anything like that. It’s more like… maybe nine months? Around there. It kind of crept in, so it’s hard to pin down exactly.\n\nI guess it got more obvious when school and work started slipping, because before that I could sort of cover it up or just tell myself I was tired. I mean, everyone’s tired in this trade, right? But yeah, not as long as I can remember. More like this past year, but not the whole year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or low most of the time, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than two weeks. It’s been kind of this long stretch, like around nine months, where I’ve felt low a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s weird because at first I could sort of hide it, or I’d blame it on being busy or tired. In the trades everyone’s always saying they’re tired, and school stuff piles up, and then you’ve got work and people expecting you to just keep moving. But once school and work started slipping, it got harder to pretend it was just normal stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a depressed mood most of the time, nearly every day, for at least two years at a time?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. I know it probably feels like I keep circling around that, sorry. It’s been more like about nine months where the low mood has been there a lot, most days.\n\nIt got harder to kind of pass off once school and work started slipping. Before that I’d just be like, “Oh, I’m tired,” or blame the schedule, because everybody does that. But… yeah, not two years. More like this stretch since sometime this past year.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or low most of the time, nearly every day, for at least two years at a time?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. I know I’ve kind of said that already, but it’s more like… around nine months.\n\nIt kind of sat there in the background for a while, and I could blame it on being busy or tired or whatever. Then once school and work started slipping, it was harder to act like nothing was going on. But no, not two years at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you’ve had no energy or that you’ve been really tired most of the time during these low mood periods?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no energy” exactly. Like, I get tired, but I also work with my hands and I’m on my feet a lot, so it’s hard to tell what’s normal tired and what’s… I don’t know, something else.\n\nSome days I’ll come home and just kind of sit there longer than I meant to, but then I think, well, anyone would be wiped after crawling around under sinks or hauling stuff. And then school stuff is its own kind of exhausting too. So… I’ve noticed being tired, yeah, but I’m not sure if it’s most of the time or if I’m just blaming everything on the schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're not good enough, or that you're a failure, even when others don't see you that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like I’m fake in the apprenticeship, like everyone else belongs and I’m just waiting for them to notice I don’t.\n\nAnd then I think about my family, or even the younger apprentices watching what I do, and I feel like I’m letting them down. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because it’s not like one mistake ends the world. But no, not two years at a time.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you’ve had no energy or that you’ve been really tired most of the time during these low mood periods?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no energy” exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s actually kind of the main thing that made me come in, I think. I keep getting this feeling like I’ve ruined things for everybody, even when, if I say it out loud, it probably sounds dramatic.

Like with the apprenticeship, I start thinking I’m a fake, like somebody’s going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And if I mess something up—something another person would probably just fix and move on from—I’ll carry it around like I let everyone down. My family, the younger apprentices, people who are counting on me to not be useless.

And I know plumbing jobs always have stuff that goes wrong. Like, that’s literally part of it, you adjust and fix it. My journeyman says that all the time, “just solve the next problem,” and he has this whole thing about not standing around staring at copper like it insulted your mother—sorry, that’s not really the point.

But yeah. I blame myself way more than makes sense. It feels like I’m disappointing everyone.

Yeah, I mean… I notice it more early in the day when I’m getting going, especially once I’m at work. It’s like my body’s got a delay on it.

So… yeah, I guess physically slowed down, especially getting started.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever noticed that your mood improves a little when something good happens, even if you're feeling down most of the time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really paid attention to it in that exact way.

Like, if something good happens, I might feel okay for a minute—like if someone at work says I did a clean job on something, or if I get coffee and it’s actually made right, which sounds stupid but some places really mess it up. There was this one gas station that had the worst coffee machine, like it tasted burned no matter what button you pressed—

Sorry. I don’t know if my mood “improves” exactly, or if I’m just distracted for a little bit. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear pattern.

Like, I get tired, but I also work with my hands and I’m on my feet a lot, so it’s hard to tell what’s normal tired and what’s… I don’t know, something else.\n\nSome days I’ll come home and just kind of sit there longer than I meant to, but then I think, well, anyone would be wiped after crawling around under sinks or hauling stuff. And then school stuff is its own kind of exhausting too. They’ll pop up, and I used to be the person who answered right away, helped organize stuff, all that. Now I see them and just kind of let them sit there. I’ll catch myself just… moving slow. Not on purpose. Mostly at work, honestly. I’ll catch myself moving slower than I mean to, like climbing down just to grab a fitting feels like this whole extra effort thing, which is ridiculous because I do that all the time.\n\nAnd sometimes the foreman asks me something simple and I take a beat too long to answer, like my mouth is catching up or something. It’s embarrassing because on a job site everyone notices when you’re off your pace. A couple people have asked if I’m sick or hungover, which… I wasn’t. So yeah, I guess it’s noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite or weight during these low mood periods?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major there. Like, that’s literally part of it, you adjust and fix it. It feels like I’m disappointing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your mood is worse in the morning, or that you feel particularly slowed down or physically heavy early in the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice it more early in the day when I’m getting going, especially once I’m at work. It’s like my body’s got a delay on it.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like climbing down to grab a fitting, and it feels like way more effort than it should. And if I mess something up—something another person would probably just fix and move on from—I’ll carry it around like I let everyone down. My family, the younger apprentices, people who are counting on me to not be useless.\n\nAnd I know plumbing jobs always have stuff that goes wrong. Like, that’s literally part of it, you adjust and fix it. My journeyman says that all the time, “just solve the next problem,” and he has this whole thing about not standing around staring at copper like it insulted your mother—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut yeah. I blame myself way more than makes sense. It feels like I’m disappointing everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your mood is worse in the morning, or that you feel particularly slowed down or physically heavy early in the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I notice it more early in the day when I’m getting going, especially once I’m at work. It’s like my body’s got a delay on it.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like climbing down to grab a fitting, and it feels like way more effort than it should. Or the foreman asks me something and I know the answer, but I answer a second too slow, like I’m loading or something. Which is not great on a job site, because everyone’s watching everyone’s pace.\n\nA couple guys have even asked if I was sick or hungover, and I wasn’t. I hate that, because then I start trying to act normal, which somehow makes me feel weirder. So… yeah, I guess physically slowed down, especially getting started.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that your mood improves a little when something good happens, even if you're feeling down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really paid attention to it in that exact way.\n\nLike, if something good happens, I might feel okay for a minute—like if someone at work says I did a clean job on something, or if I get coffee and it’s actually made right, which sounds stupid but some places really mess it up. There was this one gas station that had the worst coffee machine, like it tasted burned no matter what button you pressed—\n\nSorry. I don’t know if my mood “improves” exactly, or if I’m just distracted for a little bit. I guess I haven’t noticed a clear pattern."
